What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally got me to come in is… I’ve been arranging my whole life around not being reminded of that old job. It sounds a little strange when I say it out loud, but I avoid office buildings if I can, especially ones that look like where I used to work. If someone brings up security procedures or workplace safety talks, I’ll suddenly remember I have somewhere else to be.\n\nEven networking events—things I probably should go to for work—I won’t go if they’re near certain work sites or if I think people from that time might be there. And if anyone starts asking me about the day it happened, I just… I shut it down. I make an excuse, go to the bathroom, say I need to take a call, whatever gets me out of the conversation. It’s gotten hard to keep pretending it’s just a preference or me being busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories of that day at your old job that feel like they're happening all over again, even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… yes, that’s probably one of the worst parts, honestly, because I’m not sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something completely ordinary, like washing dishes at the sink, just running hot water and stacking plates, and then all of a sudden it’s like my mind just throws me right back into that day.\n\nOr I’ll be driving and pass one of those warehouse gates, the kind with the chain-link fence and the little guard area, and it hits me so fast I don’t even have time to brace myself. I can see pieces of it so clearly, like flashes, but they feel sharp, not like a normal memory. I can hear people’s voices again—panicked, loud, confused—and my chest gets tight like it did then. For a second it doesn’t feel like I’m in my car or standing in my kitchen. It feels like I’m back there, like my body believes it’s happening again even though part of me knows it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened at your old job when memories of it come to mind?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I think that’s most of what I do, if I’m honest. The second it starts coming up in my head, I try to shove it back down or distract myself with something—anything. I’ll get up and make myself busy, check my phone, start cleaning, turn on the TV, whatever keeps me from sitting with it.\n\nAnd it’s not just the thoughts themselves. I’ve built all these little rules around avoiding anything that might set them off. I don’t like going near office buildings that remind me of that place, and if there’s some talk about security procedures or workplace safety, I can feel myself looking for the nearest exit. I’ll suddenly decide I need coffee or the restroom, or that I forgot to make a call.\n\nNetworking events are the same. If they’re near certain job sites, or if I think someone from back then might be there, I just don’t go. I’ll tell myself it’s not important, or I’m too busy, but really I just don’t want anyone bringing it up.\n\nAnd if someone starts asking about that day directly, I shut it down fast. I don’t want to be rude, but I’ll make an excuse and leave the conversation. It’s like I’m trying to keep a door closed in my head, and if someone even touches the handle, I panic a little and get away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. I feel like I’m walking around with my nerves already pulled tight, if that makes sense. It doesn’t take much.\n\nIf a car backfires, I don’t just flinch a little—I jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. Same thing if a door slams somewhere, even in another room. Other people might look up and then go right back to what they were doing, but I’m still sitting there with my heart pounding, trying to act normal.\n\nAnd if somebody comes up behind me and touches my shoulder when I don’t see them coming, I hate that. I’ll jerk away or gasp before I can stop myself, and then I feel embarrassed because usually they didn’t mean anything by it. But my body reacts like something terrible is about to happen. It takes me a while to come down from it, longer than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling emotions related to what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I do that constantly, even if I don’t always call it that in my head. It’s like anything that might bring up the feelings from that day, I steer around it before I even have to feel it.\n\nThat’s honestly a lot of why I’m here. I keep dodging anything that even smells like that old job. Office buildings that look similar, security-related conversations, workplace safety talks, anything like that—I just feel myself wanting to get away from it. I’ll tell myself I’m being practical or that I’m just busy, but really it’s because I don’t want that feeling to come up.\n\nAnd networking events… if they’re near certain work sites, or if I think there’s any chance someone will mention that place or that day, I usually don’t go. I make an excuse. I’ll say I have another commitment, or I’m not feeling well, or it’s not worth the drive.\n\nIf someone starts asking me directly about what happened, I can feel myself almost shutting down. I don’t want to cry, I don’t want to get angry, I don’t want to feel scared in front of people, so I just make some excuse and get out of the conversation. I’ll go to the restroom, take a phone call that isn’t real, suddenly remember something I have to do. Anything to not have to sit there with those emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're to blame for what happened at your old job or that you should have done something differently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s the main thing for me. I mean… I’ve had moments where I replay things, but I’m not sure it’s exactly, “This was my fault,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes I wonder if I should’ve handled certain things differently, because that’s just how my mind works—I go back over conversations or decisions and pick them apart. But I don’t know that I walk around clearly blaming myself for what happened. It’s more… confusing than that. Like I can get stuck thinking about it, but I’m not sure I can honestly say I believe I caused it.\n\nSo, maybe a little questioning here and there, but not in a constant way, not like a firm belief that it was my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person or that you're worthless because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually think, “I’m a bad person,” exactly. And I don’t know that I’d use the word worthless. That doesn’t quite fit.\n\nBut since what happened at that job, I do catch myself thinking something changed in how I see people. Like… I don’t feel like anyone is really safe to trust anymore. Even someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you had some kind of normal working relationship with — they can turn around and become dangerous. And once that gets in your head, it’s hard to untangle from everything else.\n\nSo it’s less that I think I’m worthless, and more like I think I was foolish for trusting people the way I did before. I look back and think, how did I not see that? How did I believe things were stable or safe? And now I find myself assuming there’s always something underneath, that people can switch on you, that you can be doing your job and trying to be decent and still end up blindsided.\n\nI don’t like saying that, because it sounds so harsh. I know not everyone is dangerous. I know that logically. But emotionally, since then, it feels like trust is something I can’t really afford the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're unable to achieve your goals or have a fulfilling life because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in exactly those words, like, “I can’t have a fulfilling life.” That sounds very final, and I don’t know that I let myself go that far out loud.\n\nBut since then, I do catch myself thinking that the world is just… not as safe as I thought it was. Especially people. That’s the part that gets into everything. Because when someone you managed, or someone you tried to help, or someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with can turn around and become dangerous, it makes it hard to believe in plans the same way.\n\nSo when I think about goals, it’s not that I sit there saying, “I’ll never achieve anything.” It’s more like I don’t trust the ground under my feet anymore. I think, well, how do I build a life or move forward if I can’t even trust who’s around me? If I’m always wondering who might change, who might snap, who might not be safe?\n\nI know that probably doesn’t answer it neatly. I guess I’m saying it’s less about believing I’m incapable and more about believing that nobody is really safe to trust now. And that makes everything feel smaller, like I have to be careful with every step instead of just living.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're fundamentally changed for the worse because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so, though I don’t always put it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up every morning saying, “I’m ruined,” or anything that dramatic. But I do feel like something in me changed, and not in a good way. I used to be able to assume people were basically okay, or at least that if you treated people decently and did your job, things would stay within some kind of normal boundary. And after what happened at that job, I don’t feel that anymore.\n\nNow I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know that sounds extreme, and part of me can hear how extreme it sounds. But when someone you managed, someone you tried to help or work with like a normal person, can turn around and become dangerous… it changes how you look at everybody. It makes me second-guess even ordinary interactions, because I think, “Well, I didn’t see it coming then, so what makes me think I’d see it coming now?”\n\nSo yes, I do feel changed. I’m more guarded. I don’t like that about myself. I don’t like that my mind goes there so quickly now. It feels like I lost some part of myself that used to believe people were safe enough to deal with openly. And I don’t know how to get that back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Not really “bad person,” no. I know you’ve asked it that way a couple times, and I’m trying to answer it honestly instead of just fitting myself into the words.\n\nI don’t usually think, “I’m bad because of what happened.” That’s not the thought that loops in my head. It’s more that since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. Like if someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you were dealing with in a normal work way, can turn around and become dangerous, then what does that say about everybody else?\n\nAnd I know that sounds broad. I do know that. Part of me can step back and say, “Well, obviously not every person is unsafe.” But that’s not how it feels when I’m actually around people or thinking about what happened. The belief that sticks is more like, I was wrong to trust things as much as I did. I was wrong to assume people would stay within normal limits.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m a bad person exactly. I think I became someone who doesn’t know how to trust people anymore, and I hate that that’s where my mind goes now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently damaged or flawed because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Permanently damaged or flawed… I don’t know if I would use those exact words. “Flawed” feels like I’m saying there’s something wrong with me at the core, and I’m not sure that’s what I believe.\n\nBut I do think something changed in me after that job, and it feels hard to undo. Since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know I keep coming back to that, but that really is the thought that sits underneath everything. If someone you managed, someone you were trying to help, someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with, can turn around and become dangerous… then it makes you question everybody.\n\nIt’s like my mind says, “You didn’t know then, so how would you know now?” And that makes me feel different than I used to be. More guarded, more suspicious, less able to just take people at face value.\n\nSo, permanently damaged? I don’t want to say that, because that sounds hopeless. But it does feel like I’ve been changed in a way I can’t just shake off. The damage, if that’s the word, is that I don’t trust people the way I used to. I don’t look at people and assume they’re safe anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're fundamentally changed for the worse because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so, though I don’t always put it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up every morning saying, “I’m ruined,” or anything that dramatic. But I do feel like something in me changed, and not in a good way. I used to be able to assume people were basically okay, or at least that if you treated people decently and did your job, things would stay within some kind of normal boundary. And after what happened at that job, I don’t feel that anymore.\n\nNow I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know that sounds extreme, and part of me can hear how extreme it sounds. But when someone you managed, someone you tried to help or work with like a normal person, can turn around and become dangerous… it changes how you look at everybody. It makes me second-guess even ordinary interactions, because I think, “Well, I didn’t see it coming then, so what makes me think I’d see it coming now?”\n\nSo yes, I do feel changed. I’m more guarded. I don’t like that about myself. I don’t like that my mind goes there so quickly now. It feels like I lost some part of myself that used to believe people were safe enough to deal with openly. And I don’t know how to get that back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Not really “bad person,” no. I know you’ve asked it that way a couple times, and I’m trying to answer it honestly instead of just fitting myself into the words.\n\nI don’t usually think, “I’m bad because of what happened.” That’s not the thought that loops in my head. It’s more that since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. Like if someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you were dealing with in a normal work way, can turn around and become dangerous, then what does that say about everybody else?\n\nAnd I know that sounds broad. I do know that. Part of me can step back and say, “Well, obviously not every person is unsafe.” But that’s not how it feels when I’m actually around people or thinking about what happened. The belief that sticks is more like, I was wrong to trust things as much as I did. I was wrong to assume people would stay within normal limits.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m a bad person exactly. I think I became someone who doesn’t know how to trust people anymore, and I hate that that’s where my mind goes now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently damaged or flawed because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Permanently damaged or flawed… I don’t know if I would use those exact words. “Flawed” feels like I’m saying there’s something wrong with me at the core, and I’m not sure that’s what I believe.\n\nBut I do think something changed in me after that job, and it feels hard to undo. Since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know I keep coming back to that, but that really is the thought that sits underneath everything. If someone you managed, someone you were trying to help, someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with, can turn around and become dangerous… then it makes you question everybody.\n\nIt’s like my mind says, “You didn’t know then, so how would you know now?” And that makes me feel different than I used to be. More guarded, more suspicious, less able to just take people at face value.\n\nSo, permanently damaged? I don’t want to say that, because that sounds hopeless. But it does feel like I’ve been changed in a way I can’t just shake off. The damage, if that’s the word, is that I don’t trust people the way I used to. I don’t look at people and assume they’re safe anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because they remind you of what happened at your old job?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Trauma_Avoidance\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I keep dodging anything that even smells like that old job—office buildings, security talks, networking events near work sites—and if people start asking about the day it happened, I make some excuse and get out.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I do. Probably more than I like to admit, because some of it has become so automatic that I don’t even call it “avoiding” in my head. I just suddenly have a reason not to go, or I tell myself it’s not worth the hassle, or I take a different route.

Anything that feels too much like that old job, I tend to dodge. Office buildings especially, the kind with the same layout or the same kind of lobby, badge desk, security person sitting there… I know that sounds ordinary, but it puts me right back in that feeling. Even conversations about workplace security, or people talking about policies and what you’d do if something happened, I just want out of the room. I don’t want to sit there and hear it.

Networking events are another one. If they’re near work sites or in those business parks, I’ll find some excuse not to go. I’ll say I’m busy, or I have something with family, or I’m not feeling well. Sometimes I’ll even plan to go and then the closer it gets, I start thinking, “Why am I putting myself through this?” and I cancel.

And if people start asking about the day it happened, I really can’t stay in that conversation. I don’t always say, “I can’t talk about this.” Usually I make an excuse. I need to use the bathroom, I have to take a call, I forgot something in my car, anything like that. I just get out before they ask too much or before I have to go back through it in detail.

---------- turn 12 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally got me to come in is… I’ve been arranging my whole life around not being reminded of that old job. It sounds a little strange when I say it out loud, but I avoid office buildings if I can, especially ones that look like where I used to work. If someone brings up security procedures or workplace safety talks, I’ll suddenly remember I have somewhere else to be.\n\nEven networking events—things I probably should go to for work—I won’t go if they’re near certain work sites or if I think people from that time might be there. And if anyone starts asking me about the day it happened, I just… I shut it down. I make an excuse, go to the bathroom, say I need to take a call, whatever gets me out of the conversation. It’s gotten hard to keep pretending it’s just a preference or me being busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories of that day at your old job that feel like they're happening all over again, even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… yes, that’s probably one of the worst parts, honestly, because I’m not sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something completely ordinary, like washing dishes at the sink, just running hot water and stacking plates, and then all of a sudden it’s like my mind just throws me right back into that day.\n\nOr I’ll be driving and pass one of those warehouse gates, the kind with the chain-link fence and the little guard area, and it hits me so fast I don’t even have time to brace myself. I can see pieces of it so clearly, like flashes, but they feel sharp, not like a normal memory. I can hear people’s voices again—panicked, loud, confused—and my chest gets tight like it did then. For a second it doesn’t feel like I’m in my car or standing in my kitchen. It feels like I’m back there, like my body believes it’s happening again even though part of me knows it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened at your old job when memories of it come to mind?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I think that’s most of what I do, if I’m honest. The second it starts coming up in my head, I try to shove it back down or distract myself with something—anything. I’ll get up and make myself busy, check my phone, start cleaning, turn on the TV, whatever keeps me from sitting with it.\n\nAnd it’s not just the thoughts themselves. I’ve built all these little rules around avoiding anything that might set them off. I don’t like going near office buildings that remind me of that place, and if there’s some talk about security procedures or workplace safety, I can feel myself looking for the nearest exit. I’ll suddenly decide I need coffee or the restroom, or that I forgot to make a call.\n\nNetworking events are the same. If they’re near certain job sites, or if I think someone from back then might be there, I just don’t go. I’ll tell myself it’s not important, or I’m too busy, but really I just don’t want anyone bringing it up.\n\nAnd if someone starts asking about that day directly, I shut it down fast. I don’t want to be rude, but I’ll make an excuse and leave the conversation. It’s like I’m trying to keep a door closed in my head, and if someone even touches the handle, I panic a little and get away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. I feel like I’m walking around with my nerves already pulled tight, if that makes sense. It doesn’t take much.\n\nIf a car backfires, I don’t just flinch a little—I jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. Same thing if a door slams somewhere, even in another room. Other people might look up and then go right back to what they were doing, but I’m still sitting there with my heart pounding, trying to act normal.\n\nAnd if somebody comes up behind me and touches my shoulder when I don’t see them coming, I hate that. I’ll jerk away or gasp before I can stop myself, and then I feel embarrassed because usually they didn’t mean anything by it. But my body reacts like something terrible is about to happen. It takes me a while to come down from it, longer than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling emotions related to what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I do that constantly, even if I don’t always call it that in my head. It’s like anything that might bring up the feelings from that day, I steer around it before I even have to feel it.\n\nThat’s honestly a lot of why I’m here. I keep dodging anything that even smells like that old job. Office buildings that look similar, security-related conversations, workplace safety talks, anything like that—I just feel myself wanting to get away from it. I’ll tell myself I’m being practical or that I’m just busy, but really it’s because I don’t want that feeling to come up.\n\nAnd networking events… if they’re near certain work sites, or if I think there’s any chance someone will mention that place or that day, I usually don’t go. I make an excuse. I’ll say I have another commitment, or I’m not feeling well, or it’s not worth the drive.\n\nIf someone starts asking me directly about what happened, I can feel myself almost shutting down. I don’t want to cry, I don’t want to get angry, I don’t want to feel scared in front of people, so I just make some excuse and get out of the conversation. I’ll go to the restroom, take a phone call that isn’t real, suddenly remember something I have to do. Anything to not have to sit there with those emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're to blame for what happened at your old job or that you should have done something differently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s the main thing for me. I mean… I’ve had moments where I replay things, but I’m not sure it’s exactly, “This was my fault,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes I wonder if I should’ve handled certain things differently, because that’s just how my mind works—I go back over conversations or decisions and pick them apart. But I don’t know that I walk around clearly blaming myself for what happened. It’s more… confusing than that. Like I can get stuck thinking about it, but I’m not sure I can honestly say I believe I caused it.\n\nSo, maybe a little questioning here and there, but not in a constant way, not like a firm belief that it was my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person or that you're worthless because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually think, “I’m a bad person,” exactly. And I don’t know that I’d use the word worthless. That doesn’t quite fit.\n\nBut since what happened at that job, I do catch myself thinking something changed in how I see people. Like… I don’t feel like anyone is really safe to trust anymore. Even someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you had some kind of normal working relationship with — they can turn around and become dangerous. And once that gets in your head, it’s hard to untangle from everything else.\n\nSo it’s less that I think I’m worthless, and more like I think I was foolish for trusting people the way I did before. I look back and think, how did I not see that? How did I believe things were stable or safe? And now I find myself assuming there’s always something underneath, that people can switch on you, that you can be doing your job and trying to be decent and still end up blindsided.\n\nI don’t like saying that, because it sounds so harsh. I know not everyone is dangerous. I know that logically. But emotionally, since then, it feels like trust is something I can’t really afford the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're unable to achieve your goals or have a fulfilling life because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in exactly those words, like, “I can’t have a fulfilling life.” That sounds very final, and I don’t know that I let myself go that far out loud.\n\nBut since then, I do catch myself thinking that the world is just… not as safe as I thought it was. Especially people. That’s the part that gets into everything. Because when someone you managed, or someone you tried to help, or someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with can turn around and become dangerous, it makes it hard to believe in plans the same way.\n\nSo when I think about goals, it’s not that I sit there saying, “I’ll never achieve anything.” It’s more like I don’t trust the ground under my feet anymore. I think, well, how do I build a life or move forward if I can’t even trust who’s around me? If I’m always wondering who might change, who might snap, who might not be safe?\n\nI know that probably doesn’t answer it neatly. I guess I’m saying it’s less about believing I’m incapable and more about believing that nobody is really safe to trust now. And that makes everything feel smaller, like I have to be careful with every step instead of just living.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're fundamentally changed for the worse because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so, though I don’t always put it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up every morning saying, “I’m ruined,” or anything that dramatic. But I do feel like something in me changed, and not in a good way. I used to be able to assume people were basically okay, or at least that if you treated people decently and did your job, things would stay within some kind of normal boundary. And after what happened at that job, I don’t feel that anymore.\n\nNow I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know that sounds extreme, and part of me can hear how extreme it sounds. But when someone you managed, someone you tried to help or work with like a normal person, can turn around and become dangerous… it changes how you look at everybody. It makes me second-guess even ordinary interactions, because I think, “Well, I didn’t see it coming then, so what makes me think I’d see it coming now?”\n\nSo yes, I do feel changed. I’m more guarded. I don’t like that about myself. I don’t like that my mind goes there so quickly now. It feels like I lost some part of myself that used to believe people were safe enough to deal with openly. And I don’t know how to get that back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Not really “bad person,” no. I know you’ve asked it that way a couple times, and I’m trying to answer it honestly instead of just fitting myself into the words.\n\nI don’t usually think, “I’m bad because of what happened.” That’s not the thought that loops in my head. It’s more that since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. Like if someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you were dealing with in a normal work way, can turn around and become dangerous, then what does that say about everybody else?\n\nAnd I know that sounds broad. I do know that. Part of me can step back and say, “Well, obviously not every person is unsafe.” But that’s not how it feels when I’m actually around people or thinking about what happened. The belief that sticks is more like, I was wrong to trust things as much as I did. I was wrong to assume people would stay within normal limits.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m a bad person exactly. I think I became someone who doesn’t know how to trust people anymore, and I hate that that’s where my mind goes now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently damaged or flawed because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Permanently damaged or flawed… I don’t know if I would use those exact words. “Flawed” feels like I’m saying there’s something wrong with me at the core, and I’m not sure that’s what I believe.\n\nBut I do think something changed in me after that job, and it feels hard to undo. Since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know I keep coming back to that, but that really is the thought that sits underneath everything. If someone you managed, someone you were trying to help, someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with, can turn around and become dangerous… then it makes you question everybody.\n\nIt’s like my mind says, “You didn’t know then, so how would you know now?” And that makes me feel different than I used to be. More guarded, more suspicious, less able to just take people at face value.\n\nSo, permanently damaged? I don’t want to say that, because that sounds hopeless. But it does feel like I’ve been changed in a way I can’t just shake off. The damage, if that’s the word, is that I don’t trust people the way I used to. I don’t look at people and assume they’re safe anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because they remind you of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. Probably more than I like to admit, because some of it has become so automatic that I don’t even call it “avoiding” in my head. I just suddenly have a reason not to go, or I tell myself it’s not worth the hassle, or I take a different route.\n\nAnything that feels too much like that old job, I tend to dodge. Like I can get stuck thinking about it, but I’m not sure I can honestly say I believe I caused it.\n\nSo, maybe a little questioning here and there, but not in a constant way, not like a firm belief that it was my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person or that you're worthless because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually think, “I’m a bad person,” exactly. And I don’t know that I’d use the word worthless. That doesn’t quite fit.\n\nBut since what happened at that job, I do catch myself thinking something changed in how I see people. Like… I don’t feel like anyone is really safe to trust anymore. Even someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you had some kind of normal working relationship with — they can turn around and become dangerous. And once that gets in your head, it’s hard to untangle from everything else.\n\nSo it’s less that I think I’m worthless, and more like I think I was foolish for trusting people the way I did before. I look back and think, how did I not see that? How did I believe things were stable or safe? And now I find myself assuming there’s always something underneath, that people can switch on you, that you can be doing your job and trying to be decent and still end up blindsided.\n\nI don’t like saying that, because it sounds so harsh. I know not everyone is dangerous. I know that logically. But emotionally, since then, it feels like trust is something I can’t really afford the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're unable to achieve your goals or have a fulfilling life because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in exactly those words, like, “I can’t have a fulfilling life.” That sounds very final, and I don’t know that I let myself go that far out loud.\n\nBut since then, I do catch myself thinking that the world is just… not as safe as I thought it was. Especially people. That’s the part that gets into everything. Because when someone you managed, or someone you tried to help, or someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with can turn around and become dangerous, it makes it hard to believe in plans the same way.\n\nSo when I think about goals, it’s not that I sit there saying, “I’ll never achieve anything.” It’s more like I don’t trust the ground under my feet anymore. I think, well, how do I build a life or move forward if I can’t even trust who’s around me? If I’m always wondering who might change, who might snap, who might not be safe?\n\nI know that probably doesn’t answer it neatly. I guess I’m saying it’s less about believing I’m incapable and more about believing that nobody is really safe to trust now. And that makes everything feel smaller, like I have to be careful with every step instead of just living.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're fundamentally changed for the worse because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so, though I don’t always put it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up every morning saying, “I’m ruined,” or anything that dramatic. But I do feel like something in me changed, and not in a good way. I used to be able to assume people were basically okay, or at least that if you treated people decently and did your job, things would stay within some kind of normal boundary. And after what happened at that job, I don’t feel that anymore.\n\nNow I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know that sounds extreme, and part of me can hear how extreme it sounds. But when someone you managed, someone you tried to help or work with like a normal person, can turn around and become dangerous… it changes how you look at everybody. It makes me second-guess even ordinary interactions, because I think, “Well, I didn’t see it coming then, so what makes me think I’d see it coming now?”\n\nSo yes, I do feel changed. I’m more guarded. I don’t like that about myself. I don’t like that my mind goes there so quickly now. It feels like I lost some part of myself that used to believe people were safe enough to deal with openly. And I don’t know how to get that back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Not really “bad person,” no. I know you’ve asked it that way a couple times, and I’m trying to answer it honestly instead of just fitting myself into the words.\n\nI don’t usually think, “I’m bad because of what happened.” That’s not the thought that loops in my head. It’s more that since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. Like if someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you were dealing with in a normal work way, can turn around and become dangerous, then what does that say about everybody else?\n\nAnd I know that sounds broad. I do know that. Part of me can step back and say, “Well, obviously not every person is unsafe.” But that’s not how it feels when I’m actually around people or thinking about what happened. The belief that sticks is more like, I was wrong to trust things as much as I did. I was wrong to assume people would stay within normal limits.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m a bad person exactly. I think I became someone who doesn’t know how to trust people anymore, and I hate that that’s where my mind goes now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently damaged or flawed because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Permanently damaged or flawed… I don’t know if I would use those exact words. “Flawed” feels like I’m saying there’s something wrong with me at the core, and I’m not sure that’s what I believe.\n\nBut I do think something changed in me after that job, and it feels hard to undo. Since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know I keep coming back to that, but that really is the thought that sits underneath everything. If someone you managed, someone you were trying to help, someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with, can turn around and become dangerous… then it makes you question everybody.\n\nIt’s like my mind says, “You didn’t know then, so how would you know now?” And that makes me feel different than I used to be. More guarded, more suspicious, less able to just take people at face value.\n\nSo, permanently damaged? I don’t want to say that, because that sounds hopeless. But it does feel like I’ve been changed in a way I can’t just shake off. The damage, if that’s the word, is that I don’t trust people the way I used to. I don’t look at people and assume they’re safe anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because they remind you of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. Probably more than I like to admit, because some of it has become so automatic that I don’t even call it “avoiding” in my head. I just suddenly have a reason not to go, or I tell myself it’s not worth the hassle, or I take a different route.\n\nAnything that feels too much like that old job, I tend to dodge. Office buildings especially, the kind with the same layout or the same kind of lobby, badge desk, security person sitting there… I know that sounds ordinary, but it puts me right back in that feeling. Even conversations about workplace security, or people talking about policies and what you’d do if something happened, I just want out of the room. I don’t want to sit there and hear it.\n\nNetworking events are another one. If they’re near work sites or in those business parks, I’ll find some excuse not to go. I’ll say I’m busy, or I have something with family, or I’m not feeling well. Sometimes I’ll even plan to go and then the closer it gets, I start thinking, “Why am I putting myself through this?” and I cancel.\n\nAnd if people start asking about the day it happened, I really can’t stay in that conversation. I don’t always say, “I can’t talk about this.” Usually I make an excuse. I need to use the bathroom, I have to take a call, I forgot something in my car, anything like that. I just get out before they ask too much or before I have to go back through it in detail.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the things, so from the outside it might look like I haven’t stopped. But it’s not the same.

Before all of this, salsa music could really lift my mood. I’d put it on while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would change the whole feeling in the house. I might dance a little in the kitchen, nothing fancy, just… you know, it made me feel alive. Now I can put the same music on and I’m aware that it’s supposed to make me feel something, but it doesn’t land the same way. It’s like I hear it, but I don’t get that little spark from it.

Painting is like that too. I used to enjoy getting the colors out, setting things up, losing track of time. Now if I paint, I’m mostly just moving the brush because I told myself I should do something normal. I don’t get absorbed in it the way I used to.

And Sunday dinner… that one bothers me, because cooking Sunday dinner used to be a big thing for me. I liked planning it, feeding people, making the house smell good. Now I still cook sometimes, but it feels more like checking off a task. I go through the motions, and the good feeling just doesn’t come the way it used to. I keep waiting for it to feel comforting again, and most of the time it just doesn’t.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally got me to come in is… I’ve been arranging my whole life around not being reminded of that old job. It sounds a little strange when I say it out loud, but I avoid office buildings if I can, especially ones that look like where I used to work. If someone brings up security procedures or workplace safety talks, I’ll suddenly remember I have somewhere else to be.\n\nEven networking events—things I probably should go to for work—I won’t go if they’re near certain work sites or if I think people from that time might be there. It’s gotten hard to keep pretending it’s just a preference or me being busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories of that day at your old job that feel like they're happening all over again, even when you're not trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s… yes, that’s probably one of the worst parts, honestly, because I’m not sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something completely ordinary, like washing dishes at the sink, just running hot water and stacking plates, and then all of a sudden it’s like my mind just throws me right back into that day.\n\nOr I’ll be driving and pass one of those warehouse gates, the kind with the chain-link fence and the little guard area, and it hits me so fast I don’t even have time to brace myself. I can see pieces of it so clearly, like flashes, but they feel sharp, not like a normal memory. I can hear people’s voices again—panicked, loud, confused—and my chest gets tight like it did then. For a second it doesn’t feel like I’m in my car or standing in my kitchen. It feels like I’m back there, like my body believes it’s happening again even though part of me knows it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened at your old job when memories of it come to mind?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I think that’s most of what I do, if I’m honest. The second it starts coming up in my head, I try to shove it back down or distract myself with something—anything. I’ll get up and make myself busy, check my phone, start cleaning, turn on the TV, whatever keeps me from sitting with it.\n\nAnd it’s not just the thoughts themselves. I’ve built all these little rules around avoiding anything that might set them off. I don’t like going near office buildings that remind me of that place, and if there’s some talk about security procedures or workplace safety, I can feel myself looking for the nearest exit. I’ll suddenly decide I need coffee or the restroom, or that I forgot to make a call.\n\nNetworking events are the same. If they’re near certain job sites, or if I think someone from back then might be there, I just don’t go. I’ll tell myself it’s not important, or I’m too busy, but really I just don’t want anyone bringing it up.\n\nAnd if someone starts asking about that day directly, I shut it down fast. I don’t want to be rude, but I’ll make an excuse and leave the conversation. It’s like I’m trying to keep a door closed in my head, and if someone even touches the handle, I panic a little and get away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. I feel like I’m walking around with my nerves already pulled tight, if that makes sense. It doesn’t take much.\n\nIf a car backfires, I don’t just flinch a little—I jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. Same thing if a door slams somewhere, even in another room. Other people might look up and then go right back to what they were doing, but I’m still sitting there with my heart pounding, trying to act normal.\n\nAnd if somebody comes up behind me and touches my shoulder when I don’t see them coming, I hate that. I’ll jerk away or gasp before I can stop myself, and then I feel embarrassed because usually they didn’t mean anything by it. But my body reacts like something terrible is about to happen. It takes me a while to come down from it, longer than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling emotions related to what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I do that constantly, even if I don’t always call it that in my head. It’s like anything that might bring up the feelings from that day, I steer around it before I even have to feel it.\n\nThat’s honestly a lot of why I’m here. I keep dodging anything that even smells like that old job. Office buildings that look similar, security-related conversations, workplace safety talks, anything like that—I just feel myself wanting to get away from it. I’ll tell myself I’m being practical or that I’m just busy, but really it’s because I don’t want that feeling to come up.\n\nAnd networking events… if they’re near certain work sites, or if I think there’s any chance someone will mention that place or that day, I usually don’t go. I make an excuse. I’ll say I have another commitment, or I’m not feeling well, or it’s not worth the drive.\n\nIf someone starts asking me directly about what happened, I can feel myself almost shutting down. I don’t want to cry, I don’t want to get angry, I don’t want to feel scared in front of people, so I just make some excuse and get out of the conversation. I’ll go to the restroom, take a phone call that isn’t real, suddenly remember something I have to do. Anything to not have to sit there with those emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're to blame for what happened at your old job or that you should have done something differently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s the main thing for me. I mean… I’ve had moments where I replay things, but I’m not sure it’s exactly, “This was my fault,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes I wonder if I should’ve handled certain things differently, because that’s just how my mind works—I go back over conversations or decisions and pick them apart. But I don’t know that I walk around clearly blaming myself for what happened. It’s more… confusing than that. Like I can get stuck thinking about it, but I’m not sure I can honestly say I believe I caused it.\n\nSo, maybe a little questioning here and there, but not in a constant way, not like a firm belief that it was my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person or that you're worthless because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually think, “I’m a bad person,” exactly. And I don’t know that I’d use the word worthless. That doesn’t quite fit.\n\nBut since what happened at that job, I do catch myself thinking something changed in how I see people. Like… I don’t feel like anyone is really safe to trust anymore. Even someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you had some kind of normal working relationship with — they can turn around and become dangerous. And once that gets in your head, it’s hard to untangle from everything else.\n\nSo it’s less that I think I’m worthless, and more like I think I was foolish for trusting people the way I did before. I look back and think, how did I not see that? How did I believe things were stable or safe? And now I find myself assuming there’s always something underneath, that people can switch on you, that you can be doing your job and trying to be decent and still end up blindsided.\n\nI don’t like saying that, because it sounds so harsh. I know not everyone is dangerous. I know that logically. But emotionally, since then, it feels like trust is something I can’t really afford the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're unable to achieve your goals or have a fulfilling life because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in exactly those words, like, “I can’t have a fulfilling life.” That sounds very final, and I don’t know that I let myself go that far out loud.\n\nBut since then, I do catch myself thinking that the world is just… not as safe as I thought it was. Especially people. That’s the part that gets into everything. Because when someone you managed, or someone you tried to help, or someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with can turn around and become dangerous, it makes it hard to believe in plans the same way.\n\nSo when I think about goals, it’s not that I sit there saying, “I’ll never achieve anything.” It’s more like I don’t trust the ground under my feet anymore. I think, well, how do I build a life or move forward if I can’t even trust who’s around me? If I’m always wondering who might change, who might snap, who might not be safe?\n\nI know that probably doesn’t answer it neatly. I guess I’m saying it’s less about believing I’m incapable and more about believing that nobody is really safe to trust now. And that makes everything feel smaller, like I have to be careful with every step instead of just living.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're fundamentally changed for the worse because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so, though I don’t always put it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up every morning saying, “I’m ruined,” or anything that dramatic. But I do feel like something in me changed, and not in a good way. I used to be able to assume people were basically okay, or at least that if you treated people decently and did your job, things would stay within some kind of normal boundary. And after what happened at that job, I don’t feel that anymore.\n\nNow I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know that sounds extreme, and part of me can hear how extreme it sounds. But when someone you managed, someone you tried to help or work with like a normal person, can turn around and become dangerous… it changes how you look at everybody. It makes me second-guess even ordinary interactions, because I think, “Well, I didn’t see it coming then, so what makes me think I’d see it coming now?”\n\nSo yes, I do feel changed. I’m more guarded. I don’t like that about myself. I don’t like that my mind goes there so quickly now. It feels like I lost some part of myself that used to believe people were safe enough to deal with openly. And I don’t know how to get that back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Not really “bad person,” no. I know you’ve asked it that way a couple times, and I’m trying to answer it honestly instead of just fitting myself into the words.\n\nI don’t usually think, “I’m bad because of what happened.” That’s not the thought that loops in my head. It’s more that since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. Like if someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you were dealing with in a normal work way, can turn around and become dangerous, then what does that say about everybody else?\n\nAnd I know that sounds broad. I do know that. Part of me can step back and say, “Well, obviously not every person is unsafe.” But that’s not how it feels when I’m actually around people or thinking about what happened. The belief that sticks is more like, I was wrong to trust things as much as I did. I was wrong to assume people would stay within normal limits.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m a bad person exactly. I think I became someone who doesn’t know how to trust people anymore, and I hate that that’s where my mind goes now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently damaged or flawed because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Permanently damaged or flawed… I don’t know if I would use those exact words. “Flawed” feels like I’m saying there’s something wrong with me at the core, and I’m not sure that’s what I believe.\n\nBut I do think something changed in me after that job, and it feels hard to undo. Since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know I keep coming back to that, but that really is the thought that sits underneath everything. If someone you managed, someone you were trying to help, someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with, can turn around and become dangerous… then it makes you question everybody.\n\nIt’s like my mind says, “You didn’t know then, so how would you know now?” And that makes me feel different than I used to be. More guarded, more suspicious, less able to just take people at face value.\n\nSo, permanently damaged? I don’t want to say that, because that sounds hopeless. But it does feel like I’ve been changed in a way I can’t just shake off. The damage, if that’s the word, is that I don’t trust people the way I used to. I don’t look at people and assume they’re safe anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because they remind you of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. Probably more than I like to admit, because some of it has become so automatic that I don’t even call it “avoiding” in my head. I just suddenly have a reason not to go, or I tell myself it’s not worth the hassle, or I take a different route.\n\nAnything that feels too much like that old job, I tend to dodge. Office buildings especially, the kind with the same layout or the same kind of lobby, badge desk, security person sitting there… I know that sounds ordinary, but it puts me right back in that feeling. Even conversations about workplace security, or people talking about policies and what you’d do if something happened, I just want out of the room. I don’t want to sit there and hear it.\n\nNetworking events are another one. If they’re near work sites or in those business parks, I’ll find some excuse not to go. I’ll say I’m busy, or I have something with family, or I’m not feeling well. Sometimes I’ll even plan to go and then the closer it gets, I start thinking, “Why am I putting myself through this?” and I cancel.\n\nAnd if people start asking about the day it happened, I really can’t stay in that conversation. I don’t always say, “I can’t talk about this.” Usually I make an excuse. I need to use the bathroom, I have to take a call, I forgot something in my car, anything like that. I just get out before they ask too much or before I have to go back through it in detail.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the things, so from the outside it might look like I haven’t stopped. But it’s not the same.\n\nBefore all of this, salsa music could really lift my mood. I’d put it on while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would change the whole feeling in the house. I might dance a little in the kitchen, nothing fancy, just… you know, it made me feel alive. Now I can put the same music on and I’m aware that it’s supposed to make me feel something, but it doesn’t land the same way. It’s like I hear it, but I don’t get that little spark from it.\n\nPainting is like that too. I used to enjoy getting the colors out, setting things up, losing track of time. Now if I paint, I’m mostly just moving the brush because I told myself I should do something normal. I don’t get absorbed in it the way I used to.\n\nAnd Sunday dinner… that one bothers me, because cooking Sunday dinner used to be a big thing for me. I liked planning it, feeding people, making the house smell good. Now I still cook sometimes, but it feels more like checking off a task. I go through the motions, and the good feeling just doesn’t come the way it used to. I keep waiting for it to feel comforting again, and most of the time it just doesn’t.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I think I’m defective, exactly. That word feels… harsh. Like I’m broken as a person, and I don’t think I walk around saying that to myself in those words.

But I do think what happened changed the way I see people, and sometimes that makes me feel like there’s something wrong with how I operate now. Since then, I catch myself thinking that nobody is really safe to trust. And I hate even saying that, because it sounds so extreme, but it’s where my mind goes.

It’s like… if someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you had to deal with in a normal work way, can turn around and become dangerous, then how am I supposed to know who’s safe? I keep thinking, “Well, you didn’t see it then.” So now I second-guess myself around people. I don’t trust my own read on them the way I used to.

So inadequate? Maybe not in the sense of feeling useless or stupid. But I do feel like I lost something in myself — that ability to just believe people are basically okay until proven otherwise. Now it feels like I’m always waiting for the other shoe to drop. And that makes me feel… not defective, maybe, but definitely not like the person I was before.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally got me to come in is… I’ve been arranging my whole life around not being reminded of that old job. That’s… yes, that’s probably one of the worst parts, honestly, because I’m not sitting there trying to think about it. I’ll be doing something completely ordinary, like washing dishes at the sink, just running hot water and stacking plates, and then all of a sudden it’s like my mind just throws me right back into that day.\n\nOr I’ll be driving and pass one of those warehouse gates, the kind with the chain-link fence and the little guard area, and it hits me so fast I don’t even have time to brace myself. For a second it doesn’t feel like I’m in my car or standing in my kitchen. It feels like I’m back there, like my body believes it’s happening again even though part of me knows it isn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to push away or avoid thinking about what happened at your old job when memories of it come to mind?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, I think that’s most of what I do, if I’m honest. The second it starts coming up in my head, I try to shove it back down or distract myself with something—anything. I’ll get up and make myself busy, check my phone, start cleaning, turn on the TV, whatever keeps me from sitting with it.\n\nAnd it’s not just the thoughts themselves. I’ve built all these little rules around avoiding anything that might set them off. I don’t like going near office buildings that remind me of that place, and if there’s some talk about security procedures or workplace safety, I can feel myself looking for the nearest exit. I’ll suddenly decide I need coffee or the restroom, or that I forgot to make a call.\n\nNetworking events are the same. If they’re near certain job sites, or if I think someone from back then might be there, I just don’t go. I’ll tell myself it’s not important, or I’m too busy, but really I just don’t want anyone bringing it up.\n\nAnd if someone starts asking about that day directly, I shut it down fast. I don’t want to be rude, but I’ll make an excuse and leave the conversation. It’s like I’m trying to keep a door closed in my head, and if someone even touches the handle, I panic a little and get away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself on edge or easily startled, even when there's no apparent reason to be?\n\nPatient: Yes, very much. I feel like I’m walking around with my nerves already pulled tight, if that makes sense. It doesn’t take much.\n\nIf a car backfires, I don’t just flinch a little—I jump hard, like my whole body reacts before I even know what happened. Same thing if a door slams somewhere, even in another room. Other people might look up and then go right back to what they were doing, but I’m still sitting there with my heart pounding, trying to act normal.\n\nAnd if somebody comes up behind me and touches my shoulder when I don’t see them coming, I hate that. I’ll jerk away or gasp before I can stop myself, and then I feel embarrassed because usually they didn’t mean anything by it. But my body reacts like something terrible is about to happen. It takes me a while to come down from it, longer than it seems like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling emotions related to what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I think I do that constantly, even if I don’t always call it that in my head. It’s like anything that might bring up the feelings from that day, I steer around it before I even have to feel it.\n\nThat’s honestly a lot of why I’m here. I keep dodging anything that even smells like that old job. Office buildings that look similar, security-related conversations, workplace safety talks, anything like that—I just feel myself wanting to get away from it. I’ll tell myself I’m being practical or that I’m just busy, but really it’s because I don’t want that feeling to come up.\n\nAnd networking events… if they’re near certain work sites, or if I think there’s any chance someone will mention that place or that day, I usually don’t go. I make an excuse. I’ll say I have another commitment, or I’m not feeling well, or it’s not worth the drive.\n\nIf someone starts asking me directly about what happened, I can feel myself almost shutting down. I don’t want to cry, I don’t want to get angry, I don’t want to feel scared in front of people, so I just make some excuse and get out of the conversation. I’ll go to the restroom, take a phone call that isn’t real, suddenly remember something I have to do. Anything to not have to sit there with those emotions.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself thinking that you're to blame for what happened at your old job or that you should have done something differently?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say that’s the main thing for me. I mean… I’ve had moments where I replay things, but I’m not sure it’s exactly, “This was my fault,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nI guess sometimes I wonder if I should’ve handled certain things differently, because that’s just how my mind works—I go back over conversations or decisions and pick them apart. But I don’t know that I walk around clearly blaming myself for what happened. It’s more… confusing than that. Like I can get stuck thinking about it, but I’m not sure I can honestly say I believe I caused it.\n\nSo, maybe a little questioning here and there, but not in a constant way, not like a firm belief that it was my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person or that you're worthless because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t usually think, “I’m a bad person,” exactly. And I don’t know that I’d use the word worthless. That doesn’t quite fit.\n\nBut since what happened at that job, I do catch myself thinking something changed in how I see people. Like… I don’t feel like anyone is really safe to trust anymore. Even someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you had some kind of normal working relationship with — they can turn around and become dangerous. And once that gets in your head, it’s hard to untangle from everything else.\n\nSo it’s less that I think I’m worthless, and more like I think I was foolish for trusting people the way I did before. I look back and think, how did I not see that? How did I believe things were stable or safe? And now I find myself assuming there’s always something underneath, that people can switch on you, that you can be doing your job and trying to be decent and still end up blindsided.\n\nI don’t like saying that, because it sounds so harsh. I know not everyone is dangerous. I know that logically. But emotionally, since then, it feels like trust is something I can’t really afford the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're unable to achieve your goals or have a fulfilling life because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I think it in exactly those words, like, “I can’t have a fulfilling life.” That sounds very final, and I don’t know that I let myself go that far out loud.\n\nBut since then, I do catch myself thinking that the world is just… not as safe as I thought it was. Especially people. That’s the part that gets into everything. Because when someone you managed, or someone you tried to help, or someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with can turn around and become dangerous, it makes it hard to believe in plans the same way.\n\nSo when I think about goals, it’s not that I sit there saying, “I’ll never achieve anything.” It’s more like I don’t trust the ground under my feet anymore. I think, well, how do I build a life or move forward if I can’t even trust who’s around me? If I’m always wondering who might change, who might snap, who might not be safe?\n\nI know that probably doesn’t answer it neatly. I guess I’m saying it’s less about believing I’m incapable and more about believing that nobody is really safe to trust now. And that makes everything feel smaller, like I have to be careful with every step instead of just living.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're fundamentally changed for the worse because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so, though I don’t always put it that way in my head.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up every morning saying, “I’m ruined,” or anything that dramatic. But I do feel like something in me changed, and not in a good way. I used to be able to assume people were basically okay, or at least that if you treated people decently and did your job, things would stay within some kind of normal boundary. And after what happened at that job, I don’t feel that anymore.\n\nNow I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know that sounds extreme, and part of me can hear how extreme it sounds. But when someone you managed, someone you tried to help or work with like a normal person, can turn around and become dangerous… it changes how you look at everybody. It makes me second-guess even ordinary interactions, because I think, “Well, I didn’t see it coming then, so what makes me think I’d see it coming now?”\n\nSo yes, I do feel changed. I’m more guarded. I don’t like that about myself. I don’t like that my mind goes there so quickly now. It feels like I lost some part of myself that used to believe people were safe enough to deal with openly. And I don’t know how to get that back.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're a bad person because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Not really “bad person,” no. I know you’ve asked it that way a couple times, and I’m trying to answer it honestly instead of just fitting myself into the words.\n\nI don’t usually think, “I’m bad because of what happened.” That’s not the thought that loops in my head. It’s more that since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. Like if someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you thought you were dealing with in a normal work way, can turn around and become dangerous, then what does that say about everybody else?\n\nAnd I know that sounds broad. I do know that. Part of me can step back and say, “Well, obviously not every person is unsafe.” But that’s not how it feels when I’m actually around people or thinking about what happened. The belief that sticks is more like, I was wrong to trust things as much as I did. I was wrong to assume people would stay within normal limits.\n\nSo no, I don’t think I’m a bad person exactly. I think I became someone who doesn’t know how to trust people anymore, and I hate that that’s where my mind goes now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're permanently damaged or flawed because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Permanently damaged or flawed… I don’t know if I would use those exact words. “Flawed” feels like I’m saying there’s something wrong with me at the core, and I’m not sure that’s what I believe.\n\nBut I do think something changed in me after that job, and it feels hard to undo. Since then, I catch myself thinking nobody is really safe to trust. And I know I keep coming back to that, but that really is the thought that sits underneath everything. If someone you managed, someone you were trying to help, someone you thought you had a normal working relationship with, can turn around and become dangerous… then it makes you question everybody.\n\nIt’s like my mind says, “You didn’t know then, so how would you know now?” And that makes me feel different than I used to be. More guarded, more suspicious, less able to just take people at face value.\n\nSo, permanently damaged? I don’t want to say that, because that sounds hopeless. But it does feel like I’ve been changed in a way I can’t just shake off. The damage, if that’s the word, is that I don’t trust people the way I used to. I don’t look at people and assume they’re safe anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain activities or places because they remind you of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do. Probably more than I like to admit, because some of it has become so automatic that I don’t even call it “avoiding” in my head. I just suddenly have a reason not to go, or I tell myself it’s not worth the hassle, or I take a different route.\n\nAnything that feels too much like that old job, I tend to dodge. Office buildings especially, the kind with the same layout or the same kind of lobby, badge desk, security person sitting there… I know that sounds ordinary, but it puts me right back in that feeling. Even conversations about workplace security, or people talking about policies and what you’d do if something happened, I just want out of the room. I don’t want to sit there and hear it.\n\nNetworking events are another one. If they’re near work sites or in those business parks, I’ll find some excuse not to go. I’ll say I’m busy, or I have something with family, or I’m not feeling well. Sometimes I’ll even plan to go and then the closer it gets, I start thinking, “Why am I putting myself through this?” and I cancel.\n\nAnd if people start asking about the day it happened, I really can’t stay in that conversation. I don’t always say, “I can’t talk about this.” Usually I make an excuse. I need to use the bathroom, I have to take a call, I forgot something in my car, anything like that. I just get out before they ask too much or before I have to go back through it in detail.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy before what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I still do some of the things, so from the outside it might look like I haven’t stopped. But it’s not the same.\n\nBefore all of this, salsa music could really lift my mood. I’d put it on while I was cleaning or cooking, and it would change the whole feeling in the house. I might dance a little in the kitchen, nothing fancy, just… you know, it made me feel alive. Now I can put the same music on and I’m aware that it’s supposed to make me feel something, but it doesn’t land the same way. It’s like I hear it, but I don’t get that little spark from it.\n\nPainting is like that too. I used to enjoy getting the colors out, setting things up, losing track of time. Now if I paint, I’m mostly just moving the brush because I told myself I should do something normal. I don’t get absorbed in it the way I used to.\n\nAnd Sunday dinner… that one bothers me, because cooking Sunday dinner used to be a big thing for me. I liked planning it, feeding people, making the house smell good. Now I still cook sometimes, but it feels more like checking off a task. I go through the motions, and the good feeling just doesn’t come the way it used to. I keep waiting for it to feel comforting again, and most of the time it just doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think that you're inadequate or defective because of what happened at your old job?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I think I’m defective, exactly. That word feels… harsh. Like I’m broken as a person, and I don’t think I walk around saying that to myself in those words.\n\nBut I do think what happened changed the way I see people, and sometimes that makes me feel like there’s something wrong with how I operate now. Since then, I catch myself thinking that nobody is really safe to trust. And I hate even saying that, because it sounds so extreme, but it’s where my mind goes.\n\nIt’s like… if someone you managed, someone you tried to help, someone you had to deal with in a normal work way, can turn around and become dangerous, then how am I supposed to know who’s safe? I keep thinking, “Well, you didn’t see it then.” So now I second-guess myself around people. I don’t trust my own read on them the way I used to.\n\nSo inadequate? Maybe not in the sense of feeling useless or stupid. But I do feel like I lost something in myself — that ability to just believe people are basically okay until proven otherwise. Now it feels like I’m always waiting for the other shoe to drop. And that makes me feel… not defective, maybe, but definitely not like the person I was before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
